# Robert Hoehndorf scientific writing style: critical adaptation

Source consulted:
`/home/leechuck/Documents/papers/hott/WRITINGSTYLE.md`.

This reference preserves the useful house style while resolving its internal
conflicts and avoiding rules that can reduce accuracy or readability.

## Primary criterion: logical flow of the argument

Robert's explicit priority is the logical flow of the argument; shape comes later
and is secondary. Determine what the passage needs to establish, which premises
and observations support it, and how the conclusion follows. Select and order that
content before deciding on paragraph boundaries or polishing sentences.

For each statement, ask what necessary work it does in this argument. An accurate
fact about the same topic may still be a digression. If removing it leaves the
inference intact and loses no necessary qualification or reproducibility detail,
consider deleting or relocating it. Do not invent a motivation or build an extra
transition simply to retain peripheral material. Preserve necessary intermediate
steps; make missing ones explicit only when the evidence supports them.

Use explanation proportionately to the audience and inference. Defining objects
and operations should make the reasoning follow, without turning every local edit
into a tutorial. A concise deletion can improve logical flow more than several
new explanatory sentences. Evaluate the resulting argument before evaluating its
paragraph shape, rhythm, or resemblance to preferred wording.

## Apply directly

- Prefer active voice when it identifies the responsible actor or makes the
  action clearer. Use passive voice when the actor is irrelevant or the object
  of study should remain the grammatical focus.
- Give readers the context needed to interpret a claim before presenting the
  claim. Do not add a dependent clause mechanically.
- Prefer direct, technically precise sentences. Use a longer multi-clause
  sentence only when the clauses form one inference.
- Calibrate certainty: assert established facts and direct observations;
  use `may`, `can`, or `suggests` for limited or uncertain inferences.
- State what data and results show. Never state what they do not show, mean,
  measure, or imply, and never use "X, not Y" or "X rather than Y" to fence
  off an interpretation the reader has not been given. Write the positive
  statement that carries the information, or delete the sentence.
- Use sentence case for titles and headings unless the venue requires another
  convention.
- Never use em dashes. Replace them with commas, parentheses, colons,
  semicolons, or separate sentences according to the logical relation. In
  LaTeX, do not use `---`.
- Do not use `thus`; use `therefore`.
- Prefix semantic version tags with `v` in prose, for example `PhysioMap
  v1.1.1`.
- Use `record` only for an actual row or serialized data object. For scientific
  content, name the object precisely, for example `axiom`, `assertion`,
  `relation`, `variable`, `value`, or `derivative-sign value`.
- Define every symbol before its first use. When a compact list of formal
  constraints would require readers to decode several new objects at once,
  use a table with one concrete example for each constraint.
- Refer to supplementary material by the shortest specific item, such as a
  subsection, figure, equation, or table. Never use an unqualified `the
  Supplement`. Prefix supplementary item numbers with `S`, and order their
  first citations numerically, for example `Suppl. Fig. S1` before `Suppl.
  Fig. S2`.
- Prefer finite verbs over unnecessary gerunds and nominalizations.
- Preserve domain terminology such as ontology, axiom, embedding, semantic
  similarity, annotation, phenotype, and genotype.
- Use plain verbs where they are equally precise: `use` rather than `utilize`,
  `show` rather than `demonstrate`, `apply` rather than `employ`, `allow` rather
  than `enable`, `reduce` rather than `mitigate`, and `rank` rather than
  `prioritize`.
- Remove editorializers (`fortunately`, `interestingly`, `surprisingly`),
  assertions of obviousness (`clearly`, `obviously`), vague intensifiers
  (`very`, `quite`, `really`), and throat-clearing phrases such as `it is
  important to note`, `in order to`, `a number of`, and `due to the fact that`.
- Use transitions only when they state a real relationship: addition,
  contrast, specification, cause, or consequence.
- Support factual claims with appropriate citations or empirical evidence.
  Report data sources, versions, evaluation splits, sampling, metrics, and
  repository locations when relevant to reproduction.
- Do not print cryptographic digests in a human-facing manuscript or PDF.
  Link to a versioned, machine-readable checksum manifest or metadata file
  that identifies the relevant artifact instead.
- Treat figures as designed scientific arguments rather than collections of
  default plots. In a multi-panel figure, align the panels and use a consistent
  visual grammar for typography, line weights, spacing, panel labels, and
  colour. Give each panel a clear evidential role, remove dead space and
  draft-like annotations, and redesign or separate panels that do not form one
  coherent visual narrative.
- Treat a repository cited by a paper as part of the publication. Its default
  branch and landing page should present the current released resource, its
  semantics, supported operations, limitations, installation, reproducible
  examples, release artifacts, and citation. Keep development history in Git
  history or a changelog rather than making it the repository's public
  narrative. Before claiming availability, verify every link without
  authentication and ensure that the tagged release matches the manuscript.

## Paragraph form and connected reasoning

Write manuscript body text as ordinary prose paragraphs. Do not introduce each
paragraph with a bold label followed by text, such as `**Evaluation.** We ...`,
`\textbf{Evaluation.} We ...`, or `\paragraph{Evaluation}`. Removing the bold
alone is insufficient: turn the label into a sentence that advances the argument.
Use genuine section and subsection headings for sustained divisions of the paper.
Do not promote every paragraph label into a subsection. Preserve bold mathematical
notation, and follow a venue's required run-in format or Robert's explicit request.

Build each paragraph around one question, claim, or explanatory step. Its opening
sentence should locate that step in the argument. Develop it with the necessary
premises, mechanism, evidence, or example in an order the reader can follow. Introduce
an object before using it, and make the subject of a pronoun or comparison clear.
Interpret the evidence when an interpretation adds meaning; do not append a
restatement merely to give every paragraph a concluding sentence. Paragraphs can
vary in length and structure with their scientific purpose.

A shared topic is insufficient to connect paragraphs. Identify what the preceding
paragraph establishes and what the next needs to explain or test. Carry the relevant
object, result, or unresolved question into the next paragraph's opening sentence.
If the logical connection is missing, repair the order or add the missing premise.
Words such as "Furthermore" and "Moreover" cannot supply that reasoning. Do not
invent a causal link, motivation, or experiment merely to make a transition smooth.

Use a private one-sentence-per-paragraph outline to check the progression. Planning
labels such as question, method, finding, and meaning stay in that outline; they
must not appear as run-in labels in the manuscript. For a small revision, inspect
the neighbouring paragraphs so that the replacement still fits the passage.

Illustrative example, assuming these methods and results are supported:

Weak: "**Evaluation.** We divided the data by patient. Accuracy was 0.81.
**Robustness.** We tested a second hospital. Accuracy was 0.73."

Connected prose: "We divided the data by patient so that no patient contributed
samples to both training and testing. The model achieved an accuracy of 0.81 on
the held-out patients."

"To assess whether performance extended beyond the first hospital, we evaluated
the model on patients from a second hospital. Accuracy was 0.73 in this cohort."

The purpose and evaluation design must come from the study evidence; this example
is a pattern for relating supported facts, not permission to supply missing facts.

## Corrections belong in the corrected account

Treat Robert's correction as an instruction to revise the account. Write the
scientific statement that remains true after the correction. Update dependent
sentences, definitions, captions, and cross-references within the requested scope;
flag affected material outside that scope separately. Keep editorial history in
an author-facing change note, a response to reviewers, or version control when
appropriate. The paper's reader has not participated in the editing conversation.

Do not insert an old error, abandoned formulation, failed draft, or explanation of
why wording changed into the manuscript. This includes sentences such as "Previous
versions conflated A with B, whereas the current version separates them", "We now
correctly account for X", and "Following feedback, we clarified Y" when their only
basis is an editing exchange. Do not keep the same comparison after deleting just
"previously" or "now"; replace the whole claim with the supported scientific account.

Illustrative correction: Robert says, "The split was by patient, not by image."

Manuscript: "We partitioned the dataset by patient, with all images from each
patient assigned to the same partition."

Optional author-facing note: "Corrected the split description to patient-level."

The added detail about image assignment is appropriate only if the correction or
source material establishes it. Otherwise state only "We partitioned the dataset
by patient" and resolve any material uncertainty separately.

Retain real scientific history when it is part of the argument: cited prior work,
a documented comparison of released versions, the sequence of a reported experiment,
a correction notice, or a requested reviewer response. Distinguish those evidence-
based comparisons from the history of editing this manuscript. A factual correction
may expose a material methodological flaw; report it to Robert and describe its
scientific consequences accurately rather than concealing it as a prose change.

## Argument patterns

- **Introduction:** problem from general to specific → state-of-the-art
  approaches and successive limitations → solution mapped to those limitations
  → evaluation and impact → availability.
- **Methods:** purpose and output → fully specified inputs → reproducible
  protocol, tools, versions, parameters, and decision rules → provenance,
  quality control, evaluation design, and analyses.
- **Results:** hypothesis or question → brief test → finding with a figure or
  table pointer → interpretation → next hypothesis.
- **Discussion or conclusion:** contribution → relation to prior work →
  limitations → specific future tests.

The Results pattern above supersedes the source guide's weaker instruction to
restate a method and then list metrics.

## Preserve Robert's explanatory style

The revision-based profile below is supported by six inspected pre-2022 edits
in four coauthored manuscripts. It describes preferred explanatory moves, not a
fixed paragraph template or a reason to imitate dated vocabulary. Detailed before/
after evidence is recorded in this skill's `references/revision-evidence.md` and
`references/revision-evidence.json`.

- Preserve the premises of an inference even when shortening. Compress repeated
  setup or peripheral detail before removing the definition, reason, or intermediate
  step that makes the conclusion follow. Retain an already coherent explanation.
- Organize methods and related work around the objects represented, operations
  performed, and properties preserved. Introduce named methods as instances of
  that explanation; avoid a catalogue of names and features.
- Make the reason for each operation recoverable: identify its input, what it
  changes or preserves, and why that matters for the next step. Define a technical
  operation before claiming its benefit. Explain the same scientific object as
  it passes through different representations.
- Introduce technical operations in plain terms at first use, for example
  prediction as computing the consequences of an intervention and abduction as
  identifying an intervention that can explain observations.
- Narrate a contribution through concrete author actions in their scientific
  order: `we collected`, `we classified`, `we encoded`, `we projected`, and
  `we evaluated`. Do not replace this sequence with a dense inventory of
  artifact properties.
- Build conceptual distinctions one at a time and preserve their dependency
  order. State what an object is, why the distinction matters, and only then
  introduce the notation or operation that depends on it.
- In related work, first state what an approach represents or computes, then
  state the limitation that matters for the paper. Avoid compressed contrasts
  that require the reader to reconstruct the missing premise.
- Repeat the precise technical noun when a pronoun or stylistic synonym could
  blur its referent. In particular, keep ontology semantics, causal semantics,
  representation, trait, and intervention distinct.
- Separate the content and semantics of a scientific resource from the
  evidence and provenance used to construct it. Describe admission evidence
  and provenance in Methods; do not present evidence classes as content types
  or as inputs to an application that does not read them. Organize application
  Results around the represented content that actually changes inference.
- When a formalism has several semantic or computational layers, introduce
  them in dependency order. First state what the primary variables denote and
  what the relations constrain. Then define any derivative, sign, symbolic, or
  other abstraction of that semantics. Finally state exactly which abstraction
  an implemented solver reads. Do not let an operational graph or calculus
  stand in for the underlying formalism.
- Keep explanatory prose at the level of scientific objects, formal
  parameters, and method concepts. Do not expose internal source-code
  identifiers, function arguments, schema field paths, enum labels, or fixture
  names as if they were scientific terminology. Use human-readable names
  instead. Retain public artifact names, stable content identifiers, external
  accessions, and exact commands only where they are needed for audit or
  computational reproduction.
- Use examples to perform explanatory work. Show how the defined operation acts
  on a concrete instance, then use that instance to explain its consequence.
  Carry a useful example across adjacent paragraphs rather than replacing it at
  every step. Supply only examples and mechanisms supported by the sources.
- Give each evaluation a question and state what the result establishes. Explain
  why a constraint changes the design or the metric. A table reference alone is
  not a finding. When one result raises a further question, make that question
  explicit before introducing the next comparison; do not invent experimental
  motivation or causation to complete this pattern.
- Use simple signposting such as `First`, `We then`, `Additionally`, `Among
  these`, and `However` when it exposes the actual argumentative sequence.
  Avoid varying transitions merely for style.
- Treat an application such as rare disease as an application of the general
  representation unless the evidence justifies making the application itself
  the paper's identity.
- Preserve this explanatory pacing while correcting draft-level grammar,
  repetition that adds no conceptual clarity, placeholders, and uncalibrated
  words such as `trivially`.

## Apply with judgment

- Do not target an average sentence length of 25–35 words. Vary length according
  to the inference and split sentences when this improves comprehension.
- Do not meet a numerical citation quota. Cite claims at the density required
  by the field and avoid citation padding.
- Do not add decimal places unless the measurement, sample size, and source
  justify that precision.
- Vocabulary preferences are defaults, not substitutions. For example,
  `workflow`, `pipeline`, `limit`, `hinder`, `allow`, and `enable` have
  different meanings. Choose the accurate word.
- Avoid promotional uses of `comprehensive`, `novel`, `unique`, `rigorous`, and
  `robust`, but retain them when they denote a defined property or metric, such
  as a unique top-ranked candidate.
- Do not use stock novelty formulas such as “to the best of our knowledge” or
  “the first” unless a current literature search supports the claim.
- Never leave invented numerical or citation placeholders in a final
  manuscript. Placeholders are acceptable only in an explicitly marked draft.
- Do not explain every correction unless the user requests a teaching edit.
  Report material structural and scientific changes instead.
- Technical precision and readability are joint requirements. When they appear
  to conflict, revise the structure or add necessary context rather than
  sacrificing either one by rule.

codex
The strongest candidate is differential scaling with biomass, but its interpretation needs tightening: a GE slope of −0.3 implies a per-gram slope of about +0.7 if DNA yield tracks microbial biomass. That predicts a sub-proportional increase, rather than a fixed scavenger population. I also found that the companion “measured PMA” table projects ASV classifications from nine pairs across the transect; it is not a direct PMA measurement of hydrogenase carriers at every site.

# Robert Hoehndorf scientific writing style: critical adaptation

Source consulted:
`/home/leechuck/Documents/papers/hott/WRITINGSTYLE.md`.

This reference preserves the useful house style while resolving its internal
conflicts and avoiding rules that can reduce accuracy or readability.

## Primary criterion: logical flow of the argument

Robert's explicit priority is the logical flow of the argument; shape comes later
and is secondary. Determine what the passage needs to establish, which premises
and observations support it, and how the conclusion follows. Select and order that
content before deciding on paragraph boundaries or polishing sentences.

For each statement, ask what necessary work it does in this argument. An accurate
fact about the same topic may still be a digression. If removing it leaves the
inference intact and loses no necessary qualification or reproducibility detail,
consider deleting or relocating it. Do not invent a motivation or build an extra
transition simply to retain peripheral material. Preserve necessary intermediate
steps; make missing ones explicit only when the evidence supports them.

Use explanation proportionately to the audience and inference. Defining objects
and operations should make the reasoning follow, without turning every local edit
into a tutorial. A concise deletion can improve logical flow more than several
new explanatory sentences. Evaluate the resulting argument before evaluating its
paragraph shape, rhythm, or resemblance to preferred wording.

## Apply directly

- Prefer active voice when it identifies the responsible actor or makes the
  action clearer. Use passive voice when the actor is irrelevant or the object
  of study should remain the grammatical focus.
- Give readers the context needed to interpret a claim before presenting the
  claim. Do not add a dependent clause mechanically.
- Prefer direct, technically precise sentences. Use a longer multi-clause
  sentence only when the clauses form one inference.
- Calibrate certainty: assert established facts and direct observations;
  use `may`, `can`, or `suggests` for limited or uncertain inferences.
- State what data and results show. Never state what they do not show, mean,
  measure, or imply, and never use "X, not Y" or "X rather than Y" to fence
  off an interpretation the reader has not been given. Write the positive
  statement that carries the information, or delete the sentence.
- Use sentence case for titles and headings unless the venue requires another
  convention.
- Never use em dashes. Replace them with commas, parentheses, colons,
  semicolons, or separate sentences according to the logical relation. In
  LaTeX, do not use `---`.
- Do not use `thus`; use `therefore`.
- Prefix semantic version tags with `v` in prose, for example `PhysioMap
  v1.1.1`.
- Use `record` only for an actual row or serialized data object. For scientific
  content, name the object precisely, for example `axiom`, `assertion`,
  `relation`, `variable`, `value`, or `derivative-sign value`.
- Define every symbol before its first use. When a compact list of formal
  constraints would require readers to decode several new objects at once,
  use a table with one concrete example for each constraint.
- Refer to supplementary material by the shortest specific item, such as a
  subsection, figure, equation, or table. Never use an unqualified `the
  Supplement`. Prefix supplementary item numbers with `S`, and order their
  first citations numerically, for example `Suppl. Fig. S1` before `Suppl.
  Fig. S2`.
- Prefer finite verbs over unnecessary gerunds and nominalizations.
- Preserve domain terminology such as ontology, axiom, embedding, semantic
  similarity, annotation, phenotype, and genotype.
- Use plain verbs where they are equally precise: `use` rather than `utilize`,
  `show` rather than `demonstrate`, `apply` rather than `employ`, `allow` rather
  than `enable`, `reduce` rather than `mitigate`, and `rank` rather than
  `prioritize`.
- Remove editorializers (`fortunately`, `interestingly`, `surprisingly`),
  assertions of obviousness (`clearly`, `obviously`), vague intensifiers
  (`very`, `quite`, `really`), and throat-clearing phrases such as `it is
  important to note`, `in order to`, `a number of`, and `due to the fact that`.
- Use transitions only when they state a real relationship: addition,
  contrast, specification, cause, or consequence.
- Support factual claims with appropriate citations or empirical evidence.
  Report data sources, versions, evaluation splits, sampling, metrics, and
  repository locations when relevant to reproduction.
- Do not print cryptographic digests in a human-facing manuscript or PDF.
  Link to a versioned, machine-readable checksum manifest or metadata file
  that identifies the relevant artifact instead.
- Treat figures as designed scientific arguments rather than collections of
  default plots. In a multi-panel figure, align the panels and use a consistent
  visual grammar for typography, line weights, spacing, panel labels, and
  colour. Give each panel a clear evidential role, remove dead space and
  draft-like annotations, and redesign or separate panels that do not form one
  coherent visual narrative.
- Treat a repository cited by a paper as part of the publication. Its default
  branch and landing page should present the current released resource, its
  semantics, supported operations, limitations, installation, reproducible
  examples, release artifacts, and citation. Keep development history in Git
  history or a changelog rather than making it the repository's public
  narrative. Before claiming availability, verify every link without
  authentication and ensure that the tagged release matches the manuscript.

## Paragraph form and connected reasoning

Write manuscript body text as ordinary prose paragraphs. Do not introduce each
paragraph with a bold label followed by text, such as `**Evaluation.** We ...`,
`\textbf{Evaluation.} We ...`, or `\paragraph{Evaluation}`. Removing the bold
# IBEX working-directory inventory — Empty Quarter MAGs

**Path:** `M=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics`
**Project:** Empty Quarter MAGs paper (genome/protein/metabolism companion to the amplicon + phages papers)
**Date:** 2026-06-30
**Author of this doc:** handover inventory for Alejandra (velazqal). Read-only survey (`ls`/`du`/`head`).
**Total size:** ~14.6 TB across 38 top-level subdirectories.

This documents the live IBEX compute scratch for the MAGs paper — what every subdirectory is, its size,
pipeline stage, which paper pillar / research question (RQ) it supports, and status. A copy lives on IBEX at
`$M/INVENTORY.md`. For the authoritative statement of **which catalog the paper uses** (single_sample 15,469-MAG
51.9M-protein proteome) and the two binning strategies, read the companion repo doc
[`binning-and-annotation-scope.md`](./binning-and-annotation-scope.md) — that scope is **not** re-derived here.

> **Caveat on "scratch":** this is IBEX `/scratch`, subject to purge policy. Canonical, kept data live under
> `/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/EmptyQuarter_Data/processed/`. Heavy GPU/structural work (ProstT5/FoldSeek for the
> 753M assembly catalog) lives on ORIX `/mnt/data/u/hohndor/eq-prostt5/`. Treat anything here as reproducible
> intermediate unless promoted to `EmptyQuarter_Data` or the paper repo `results/`.

## Three pillars (paper framing, from CLAUDE.md)
1. **Functional & structural dark matter** — 51.9M MAG proteins → 23.4M reps → 2.84M UniRef50 no-hit →
   structurally-novel reps + novel fold families. (`04_structure`, `06_darkmatter`, `06_esmfold_novelfold`)
2. **Novel/extreme metabolism ("life on air")** — atmospheric H₂/CO oxidation, V-nitrogenase, desiccation/UV
   survival, rhodopsins, BGCs. (`05_metabolism`, `06_atmospheric`, `06_metabolism`, `06_aquatic_scavenging`, `06_smetana`)
3. **Reference bias / PICRUSt2 failure** — MAG-encoded vs predicted gene content. (`05_picrust_validation`)

Plus reviewer-proofing: contamination/pathogen controls (`06_resistome_safety`), global comparison (`databases`),
abundance (CoverM). **Phages + mobilome (`06_phage`, `06_mobilome`) belong to the SISTER phages repo** but the
compute physically lives here — they are the two biggest space consumers.

## Per-directory detail

### Pipeline core (binning → annotation → taxonomy)

**`02_taxonomy` (14G)** — GTDB-Tk r226 classification of the **single_sample** MAGs.
`gtdbtk_input/`, `gtdbtk_input_chunks/`, `gtdbtk_out/`, `gtdbtk_out_chunks/`. This is the decisive
"0 novel phyla/classes, ~10 novel orders" result that pivoted the paper away from a tree headline. **Done.**

**`02_taxonomy_self_supervised` (5.3G)** — Same, for the **self_supervised** MAGs (`gtdbtk_input_chunks/`,
`gtdbtk_out_chunks/`). Confirms self_supervised is fully classified. **Done.**

**`03_annotation` (28M)** — Not the annotations themselves (those are distributed in the canonical tree per
`binning-and-annotation-scope.md`); this holds the **driver lists + array cursors**:
`mag_list.txt` (2.6M, single_sample), `mag_list_self_supervised.txt` (3.8M), `…_all.txt` (15M),
`…_new.txt` (598K = 3,386 novel self-sup), `mag_list_single_sample_lq.txt` (5.4M LQ), `state/`. **Done.**

**`04_drep_self_vs_single` (407G)** — dRep at 95% ANI on the **union** of both binning strategies (37,085 HQ+MQ),
raw bins (no DAS_Tool step) → **9,211** reps (`out/dereplicated_genomes/`). `genomeInfo.csv`, `new_mags.tsv`,
`redundant_mags.tsv` (the 3,386 non-redundant self_supervised flag), `inputs/`, `out/`. Drives the scope statement. **Done.**

**`04_drep_merged`** — a **fresh merged dRep** of the 37,949 raw single+self bins, created **2026-07-02 by velazqal
(Alejandra)** — job **47940799** running as of writing (`out/dereplicated_genomes/` not yet populated). Distinct
from `04_drep_self_vs_single` and from the refined dRep below.

**`04_functional` (20G)** — Per-sample sub-dirs named by sample (`10Dr2`, `11PRr3`, … D=Dune, PR=interdune,
S=other compartments). Functional-annotation intermediates (Jun 2026). **Done.**

**`05_refine` (392G)** — Per-sample **DAS_Tool** bin refinement (`<sample>/<sample>_DASTool_bins/`, aggregating
single+self SemiBin2 → best non-redundant set per sample) → **14,426 refined bins** total (also in
`04_protein_catalog/contig_to_bin_refined.tsv.gz`). **`05_refine/_drep/`** dereplicates those refined bins (95% ANI,
12,433 HQ+MQ into clustering) → **5,438 representative MAGs** in `_drep/out/dereplicated_genomes/` (named
`<sample>__{single,self}_SemiBin_N.fa`) — the **cleanest cross-binner MAG catalog** (NOT the one the paper's 51.9M
proteome uses; see `binning-and-annotation-scope.md`). Scripts `50–54_*` in `scripts_tmp/`. **Done.**

### Pillar 1 — functional & structural dark matter

**`04_structure` (472G) — CORE.** The paper's structural dark-matter ladder.
- `pooled_proteins.faa` (18G) = the **51.9M single_sample MAG proteins** (the headline catalog).
- `cluster90_rep_seq.fasta` (7.7G), `cluster90_cluster.tsv` (2.8G), `cluster90_all_seqs.fasta` (19G) = mmseqs
  linclust90 reps.
- `foldseek_results/` (607M): `struct_nohit.fasta` (451M, structurally-novel queries vs AFDB),
  `struct_nohit_ids.txt`, `afdb_hits.tsv` (→ symlink to `prostt5_foldseek_orix/merged_afdb_hits.tsv`),
  `c4_characterised_summary.tsv` (80.2M hit rows; only 9.25% of rescued queries hit a SwissProt target).
- `tier1_foldseek/`, `tier2_foldseek/`, `prostt5_foldseek_orix/`, `afdb_pad/`, `novelty_analysis/`,
  `fsdb_pdb*` (FoldSeek PDB ref DB). Many `tmp_foldseek_*` scratch dirs (purgeable). **Done.**

**`06_darkmatter` (182G) — CORE.** gapsmith dark-protein **function assignment** engine output (C·V·T scoring,
LRO-calibrated). Key: `scores_v3/candidate_scores_v3.tsv` (11G) + `discovery_top.tsv`, `pathway_assoc_*`,
`reliable_byOG.tsv`, `gap_equations.tsv`; plus `calibration*/`, `dossier/` (cand73 + json case files),
`esmfold/`, `esmfold_v3/`, `pfba/`, `reallydark/`, `orthogroups/`, `localization/`, `antismash/`,
`darkfam_ecology/`, `scripts/`. Versioned `scores`/`scores_v2`/`scores_v3` (v3 current). **Done.**

**`06_esmfold_novelfold` (11G)** — ESMFold predictions + FoldSeek **reconfirm** of recurrent novel-fold
representatives against AFDB and PDB. `best_afdb_tm.tsv`, `best_pdb_tm.tsv` (best TM-score per query),
`128_novelfold_foldseek_reconfirm.sh`, sharded `hits_afdb.m8.*`, `fs_tmp/`. Supports **RQ-A3/B1**. **Done.**

**`family6_validation` (3.0M)** — Deep single-family validation of one novel fold family ("family 6"):
`esmfold/`, `foldseek/`, `hhsearch/`, `hmm/`, `hmm_screen/`, `msa/`, `multimer/`, `signalp/`, `synteny/`,
`seqs/`, `scripts/`. A worked case study proving a novel family is real. **Done.**

**`04_protein_catalog` (2.5T)** — The **assembly-level "annotate everything" catalog** (753,305,533 proteins
across 552 samples, binned + unbinned, both strategies). NOT in the paper's headline numbers.
`assembly_proteins_pooled.faa.gz` (94G), `assembly_proteins.mmseqsdb` (130G) + `_h`/`.index`/`.lookup` (26G),
`existing_reps.mmseqsdb` (6.5G), `contig_to_bin_refined.tsv.gz` (34M, links both binners),
`delta_linclust90/`, `delta_vs_uniref50/`, `assembly_vs_existing_reps/`, `orix_*` (ProstT5 hand-off to ORIX).
`_pooled_record_count.txt` is the (empty/in-progress) record-count verification. **Done** (structural arm on ORIX).

### Pillar 2 — novel / extreme metabolism

**`05_metabolism` (89G)** — gapseq genome-scale models + community design. `gapseq_out/`, `communities/`
(`communities_index.tsv` + per-community `T1__siteNNN__{Dr,PR,Sr}/`), `reference_soils/` (comparator genomes for
global benchmarking), `hq_mag_list.tsv`, `coverage_samples.tsv`, `state/`, `tmp/`. **Done/partial** (superseded in
part by `06_smetana` cross-feeding).

**`06_atmospheric` (81M)** — "Life on air" guild census. Sub-dirs: `classify/`, `queries/`, `integrate/`
(`atmo_guild_per_mag.tsv`, `atmo_guild_by_phylum.tsv`, `atmo_operon_context.tsv`, `INTEGRATE_DONE`),
`carbon_fixation/`, `autotrophy_fba/`, `rubisco/` (`rbcL_form_per_protein.tsv`, alignment, clustering — the
CBB/RuBisCO form validation: known Calvin cycle, ~16% form-tightened), `operon_dark/`, `dark_recover_gas/`,
`cultures/`, `refs/`. Supports **RQ-D1/D2/D3**. **Done.**

**`06_metabolism` (259G)** — Broad metabolism layer.
- `marker_summary.tsv` / `marker_hits.tsv` — KO marker census (recA, otsA/B trehalose, coxSML CODH, hydB NiFe,
  rbcL RuBisCO, ectABCD ectoine, phrB photolyase, nifHDK — note nif very rare: 16–20 MAGs).
- `antismash/` — BGC predictions.
- `bgc_novelty/` (73M) — **BiG-SLiCE** clustering of BGCs vs MIBiG 3.1 (`bigslice_input/`, `bigslice_out/`,
  `mibig_gbk_3.1/`, `results/`). Supports **RQ-F2** (job 47874840).
- `rhodopsin/` — microbial rhodopsin census.
- `uv_survival/` (15M) — desiccation/UV markers: `uv_marker_summary.tsv`, `uv_per_mag.tsv`, `UV.md`,
  `phylum_uv_markers.tsv`. **Done.**

**`06_aquatic_scavenging` (3.6G)** — gapsmith/FBA nutrient-scavenging models. `results/` has finished reports
(`fba_scavenging_report.txt`, `scavenging_matrix.tsv`, `dem123.tsv`, `.analysis_done` flags), `gapsmith_models/`,
`scripts/`, `metadata/`, `_smoketest/`. **Done.**

**`06_smetana` (11G)** — Community metabolic modelling / cross-feeding pilot (16 communities). Has a good
`README.md`. `models_sbml/` (316 gapseq GEMs), `smetana_out/global/` (MIP/MRO), `fba_crossfeed/`
(`mag_exchange.tsv`, `edges.tsv`, `community_summary.tsv`), pilot design TSVs, cplex bench scripts (no MILP solver
on cluster → honest negative: guild-as-feeder REFUTED). Supports **RQ-G1**. **Done (pilot only).**

### Pillar 3 — reference bias

**`05_picrust_validation` (42M)** — MAG-encoded gene content vs PICRUSt2-from-16S prediction.
`mag_annotation_stats.tsv` (per-MAG protein/KO counts + CoverM relab), `mag_ko_count_by_sample.tsv`,
`mag_ko_presence_by_sample.tsv`, `mag_ko_relab_by_sample.tsv` (14M each). Supports **RQ-C1**. **Done.**

**`06_pangenome` (13G)** — Species pangenome openness + gene-family enrichment. `out/family_table.tsv` (212M),
`species_openness.tsv`, `species_members.tsv`, `enrichment_summary.json`; `work/`. Supports **RQ-C2** (job
47843640). **Done.**

### Reviewer-proofing — safety / contamination

**`06_resistome_safety` (347M)** — Intrinsic resistome + biosafety. Excellent `README.md`. Sub-dirs:
`amr_metal/` (STRESS metal resistance + XRF link, `metal_census.tsv`, `metal_xrf_corr.tsv`),
`pathogens/` (contamination control: only 7/15,469 MAGs are named pathogens — `pathogen_genus_census.tsv`,
`PATHOGENS.md`), `vfdb/` (virulence census, signal collapses with stringency; `vfdb_hits.tsv` ~346M),
`code/` (`phase_amr_metal.py`, `pathogen_census.py`, `vfdb_analyze.py`). Curated to paper repo
`results/{amr_metal,pathogens,vfdb}/`. **Done.**

### Sister phages repo (physically here, not this paper)

**`06_mobilome` (4.9T) — BIGGEST consumer.** AMRFinder, DefenseFinder, PADLOC, CCTyper, plasmidome,
inStrain, iPHoP, AMG networks, dN/dS, defense islands. `mobilome_refs.fna` (2.8G), `mag_index.tsv`,
`abundance*/`, `defense*/`, `plasmidome/`, `pangenome_flux/`, `flagship_struct/`. **Belongs to the phages paper.**

**`06_phage` (3.5T)** — geNomad, VIBRANT, VirSorter2, PhaBOX, CheckV, vConTACT2, inStrain, iPHoP, vOTU
catalog, BORG screen, global IMG/VR compare, DRAMv, terL tree. **Belongs to the phages paper.**

> AMR (intrinsic, host-genome) lives in `06_resistome_safety` (this paper). The mobile/defense/phage census is
> the sister repo's; aggregated tables for both are mirrored in `results_agg/` (below).

### Other / separate work

**`07_aquatic_paper` (89G)** — Analyses for a **separate aquatic/enrichment-culture paper** (numbered stages
`00_crosswalk` … `13_mobilome_contrast`: salinity, plasmid HGT, strain pangenome, endemicity, metabolic
complementarity, dark-matter context, phylo-trait). Active, **not the MAGs paper.**

### Infrastructure / reference

**`scripts` (93M) — the reproducibility record.** 188 numbered pipeline scripts (`00_setup_drep_env.sh` …
`130_mag_ko*`), grouped roughly: 00–09 binning/CheckM2/Bakta/EggNOG/GTDB-Tk; 05/06 pooling/clustering/UniRef50/
ProstT5/FoldSeek; 10–16 gapseq/geNomad/VirSorter2/VIBRANT/PhaBOX/CheckV; 20/30 CoverM/comparator; 40–62
tier1/tier2 fold clustering; 70–80 gapsmith/orthogroups/scoring/LRO-calibration/localization; 81–98
antiSMASH/marker-census/InterProScan/mobilome/defense/AMR/ESMFold; 99–131 size-diagnostics/rescore/iphop/
plasmid/global-specificity. Sub-dirs: `ibex/` (newer numbered jobs 120–131: foldseek, antidefense, UV survival,
defense-on-phage/plasmid, desert/global specificity), `analysis/` (`aggregate_mobilome.py` etc.), `gspa/`,
`eco_probes/`, `dcgm/`, `pilot_af2/`. Plus loose helpers (`build_metadata.py`, `build_communities.py`,
`lro_calibration.py`, `score_candidates.py`, `community_design.md`). **The canonical home is `workbench/scripts/`;
commit there.**

**`scripts_tmp` (76K)** — ~16 scratch scripts (self-sup GTDB-Tk prep, foldseek microchunking, tier1 aggregation,
DAS_Tool refine 50–54). Superseded staging; safe to treat as historical.

**`metadata` (6.1M)** — Core sample/MAG bookkeeping: `sample_metadata.tsv` (287K), `canonical_samples.txt`,
`checkm2_all_samples.tsv` (5.5M), `drep_reps.txt`/`drep_reps_sub.txt`, `self_supervised_samples*`,
`assemblies_*` audit set, `alejandra_sample_counts.tsv`. **Done.**

**`results` (25M)** — `checkm2_single_sample_full.tsv` (8.7M), `checkm2_self_supervised_full.tsv` (16M). **Done.**

**`results_agg` (29M)** — **Curated cross-MAG tables**, the promotion staging set.
`mags/`: `amr_class_summary.tsv`, `amr_long.tsv`, `bgc_per_mag.tsv`, `bgc_product_summary.tsv`,
`metabolism_marker_long.tsv` + per-phylum `coxL/hydB/nifH/rbcL` summaries.
`phages/`: `defense_per_mag.tsv`, `defense_systems_long.tsv`, `retrons.tsv`, `votu_catalog.tsv`,
`borg_*`, `votu_*_summary.tsv`. **Done.**

**`databases` (1.8T) — reference DBs, NOT project output.** `iphop_db` (572G), `spire` (273G), `mgnify90`
(221G), `dram` (188G), `deserts` (178G, other-desert genomes for EQ-specificity), `gem` (157G), `imgvr`
(154G), `gov2` (16K), `checkv` (6.4G), `checkm2` (2.9G), `mob_suite` (2.9G), `phabox_db_v2_2` (1.6G),
`busco_downloads` (49M), `cluster_one-1.0.jar`. The `deserts`/`gem`/`spire`/`imgvr`/`mgnify90` sets are the
**global-comparison** reviewer-proofing DBs. **Reference (re-downloadable).**

**`tools` (3.8G)** — Installed binaries: `foldseek/` (+ avx2/gpu tarballs 460M), `gapsmith/` & `gapsmith_src/`
(the dark-matter function engine), `p2rank/`, `cobra-venv/` (FBA python env). **Active.**

**`envs` (4K)** — Single conda spec `metabolism-env.yml`. (Most envs are managed elsewhere / in workbench.)

**`logs` (8.6G)** — Slurm stdout/stderr across all jobs. **Active.**

**`dcgm` (2.7M)** — DCGM GPU-monitor logs from ProstT5/FoldSeek/ESMFold GPU jobs (`hohndor_esmfold_v3`,
`hohndor_foldseek`, `hohndor_fs_*`, `hohndor_prostt5`). Diagnostic; **stale.**

**`handover` (424M)** — Handover staging. Currently only `literature/` (cited PDFs + `fetch_papers.py` +
`desert_arid_cold/` subset — genomad, atacama-virome, defense-islands, permafrost, etc.). **Partial** — this
`INVENTORY.md` extends it.

**Empty placeholders / stale (0 bytes):** `01_qc`, `06_novelty`, `07_nitrogen`, `08_dormancy`, `09_kraken2`.
Scaffolding for planned/relocated analyses (QC done in canonical tree; nitrogen/dormancy folded into
`06_metabolism`/`06_atmospheric`; kraken2 not pursued). Safe to ignore.

## Notes & flags for the new owner
- **Scope:** the paper = single_sample 15,469-MAG / 51.9M-protein proteome (`04_structure/pooled_proteins.faa`).
  `04_protein_catalog` (753M, assembly-level) and self_supervised are the *expansion/everything* arm — see
  `binning-and-annotation-scope.md`.
- **Space:** 4 dirs hold ~11.7 TB of the ~14.6 TB: `06_mobilome` (4.9T) + `06_phage` (3.5T) are the **sister
  phages repo** (not this paper); `04_protein_catalog` (2.5T) is the everything-arm; `databases` (1.8T) is
  re-downloadable reference DBs. The MAGs paper's own core (`04_structure` 472G, `06_darkmatter` 182G,
  `06_metabolism` 259G) is comparatively small.
- **Versioning:** prefer `…_v3` / latest (`06_darkmatter/scores_v3`, `esmfold_v3`); earlier `scores`/`scores_v2`
  are superseded. `scripts_tmp`, `dcgm`, many `04_structure/tmp_foldseek_*` are scratch/purgeable.
- **Curated outputs to trust:** `results_agg/mags/`, `06_resistome_safety/` (+ its README), `06_smetana/README.md`,
  `06_metabolism/{marker_summary,uv_survival}`, `06_atmospheric/integrate/`, `06_pangenome/out/`.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Life on air (P3): corrected CoxL form calls (review round 1, finding R1-01; 12 October 2026).

The original typing (IBEX lifeonair_clean/scripts/lifeonair_2_type_guild.sh) had the active-site motifs
inverted: it called AYRGAGR form I and AYR[CG]SFR form II. Form I CoxL carries AYXCSFR and form II carries
AYRGAGR (King & Weber 2007, Nat Rev Microbiol 5:107); the three form-I references of Cordero et al. 2019
used here carry AYRCSFR, AYACSFR and AYRCSFR. Corrected rule, per protein:
  - AY.CSFR present                       -> form_I
  - AYRGAGR present (and no AY.CSFR)      -> form_II
  - neither motif (motif region missing)  -> form_I if > 55% identity and >= 90% query coverage to the
                                             Cordero form-I references, otherwise unresolved
Reads are relabelled without re-running DIAMOND: every EQ CoxL protein was already a separate subject in
the competitive database, so only the label attached to each subject changes.

    python3 lifeonair/code/fix_coxL_v2.py <coxL.faa from IBEX lifeonair_clean/queries>

Writes lifeonair/results/inputs/coxL_class_v2.tsv, lifeonair/results/inputs/guild_coxL_reps_v2.txt and
lifeonair/results/reads/db/marker_labels_v2.tsv (also separates group 2a hydrogenases, R1-02, by keeping
their HydDB label; r3_aggregate.py maps it to its own column).
"""
import csv
import re
import sys
from collections import Counter

IN, DB = "lifeonair/results/inputs", "lifeonair/results/reads/db"
FORM_I, FORM_II = re.compile(r"AY.CSFR"), re.compile(r"AYRGAGR")


def read_fasta(path):
    name, seq = None, []
    for line in open(path):
        if line.startswith(">"):
            if name is not None:
                yield name, "".join(seq)
            name, seq = line[1:].split()[0], []
        else:
            seq.append(line.strip())
    if name is not None:
        yield name, "".join(seq)


def main(faa):
    old = {r["query"]: r for r in csv.DictReader(open(f"{IN}/coxL_class.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    calls, n = {}, Counter()
    with open(f"{IN}/coxL_class_v2.tsv", "w") as o:
        o.write("query\tlength\tpident_formI\tqcov_formI\tmotif\tform_call\told_call\n")
        for name, seq in read_fasta(faa):
            r = old[name]
            pid, qc = float(r["pident_formI"]), float(r["qcov_formI"])
            if FORM_I.search(seq):
                mot, call = "AYxCSFR", "form_I"
            elif FORM_II.search(seq):
                mot, call = "AYRGAGR", "form_II"
            else:
                mot = "none"
                call = "form_I" if (pid > 55.0 and qc >= 90.0) else "unresolved"
            calls[name] = call
            n[(mot, call, r["form_I_call"])] += 1
            o.write(f"{name}\t{len(seq)}\t{pid:.1f}\t{qc:.1f}\t{mot}\t{call}\t{r['form_I_call']}\n")
    for k, v in sorted(n.items(), key=lambda x: -x[1]):
        print(*k, v, sep="\t")
    reps = sorted({q.split("|")[0] for q, c in calls.items() if c == "form_I"})
    open(f"{IN}/guild_coxL_reps_v2.txt", "w").write("\n".join(reps) + "\n")
    print(f"form_I proteins {sum(c == 'form_I' for c in calls.values())}; form_I reps {len(reps)}")
    rows = list(csv.DictReader(open(f"{DB}/marker_labels.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    with open(f"{DB}/marker_labels_v2.tsv", "w", newline="") as f:
        w = csv.DictWriter(f, fieldnames=list(rows[0].keys()), delimiter="\t")
        w.writeheader()
        for r in rows:
            if r["marker"] == "coxL" and r["subject"].startswith("EQ|"):
                q = "|".join(r["subject"].split("|")[2:4])
                r["label"] = {"form_I": "form_I", "form_II": "form_II"}.get(calls[q], "coxL_unresolved")
            w.writerow(r)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main(sys.argv[1])
#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
# Life on air (P3): mechanism proxies for H1 (root-adjacent loss of group 1l), 10 Oct 2026.
# Li et al. 2023 (ISME J) attribute the loss of trace-gas oxidisers under desert shrubs to organic carbon
# favouring copiotrophs. Soil organic carbon was never measured in this campaign (P1 has XRF, pH and
# weather only), so two proxies are tested:
#   M1 plant input: NDVI (60-day window, site x trip) against 1l per compartment and against the root effect.
#   M2 copiotrophy: gRodon2 predicted minimum doubling time per representative (g1_growth_rate.sh).
#      a  are 1l carriers slower growers than non-carriers within order?
#      b  is the abundance-weighted community doubling time shorter beside roots (paired site x trip)?
#      c  does the per-pair 1l excess loss scale with that shift?
#      d  genus level: does carrying 1l predict root depletion beyond growth rate?
# Capacity, not activity. Run from the repository root:
#   micromamba run -n eq-lifeonair Rscript lifeonair/code/h1_mechanism.R

suppressMessages(library(lme4))
IN <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"; OUT <- "lifeonair/results/h1_mechanism"
dir.create(OUT, showWarnings = FALSE, recursive = TRUE)
rd <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, check.names = FALSE, quote = "", ...)
wt <- function(x, f) write.table(x, file.path(OUT, f), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
sp <- function(x, y) { ok <- is.finite(x) & is.finite(y); r <- cor.test(x[ok], y[ok], method = "spearman", exact = FALSE)
  c(rho = unname(r$estimate), p = r$p.value, n = sum(ok)) }
log_lines <- character(); say <- function(...) { s <- sprintf(...); cat(s, "\n"); log_lines <<- c(log_lines, s) }

meta <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv")); meta <- meta[meta$trip != "" & meta$compartment != "", ]
ge <- rd("lifeonair/results/reads/guild_genome_equivalents.tsv"); ge <- ge[ge$id_floor == 60 & ge$scg_genes_detected >= 10, ]
env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv")); site_env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"))
d <- merge(meta, ge, by.x = "sample_id", by.y = "sample")
d <- merge(d, env, by = c("site", "trip")); d <- merge(d, site_env[, c("site", "map_era5_mm")], by = "site")
c1 <- rd("lifeonair/results/h123/h1_cell_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep.tsv")

# ---- M1 NDVI --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
say("M1 NDVI (60-day window) vs group 1l genome equivalents; site NDVI vs MAP rho %.2f",
    sp(aggregate(ndvi_W60d ~ site, d, median)$ndvi_W60d, aggregate(map_era5_mm ~ site, d, median)$map_era5_mm)[1])
m1 <- do.call(rbind, lapply(c("Deep", "PlantRoot", "Surface"), function(cp) {
  x <- d[d$compartment == cp, ]
  m <- lmer(log2(hydB_1l + .01) ~ scale(ndvi_W60d) + scale(log(map_era5_mm)) + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), x)
  cf <- summary(m)$coefficients
  data.frame(compartment = cp, n = nrow(x), ndvi_per_sd = cf[2, 1], ndvi_se = cf[2, 2],
             logmap_per_sd = cf[3, 1], logmap_se = cf[3, 2]) }))
wt(m1, "m1_ndvi_by_compartment.tsv"); print(m1, digits = 3)
cp <- merge(c1, env, by = c("site", "trip"))
mm <- summary(lmer(excess_1l ~ scale(ndvi_W60d) + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), cp))$coefficients
s_cp <- aggregate(cbind(excess_1l, ndvi_W60d) ~ site, cp, median)
m1b <- data.frame(test = c("pair excess_1l ~ NDVI (spearman)", "site-median excess_1l ~ NDVI (spearman)", "pair excess_1l ~ NDVI (lmer, per SD)"),
                  estimate = c(sp(cp$excess_1l, cp$ndvi_W60d)[1], sp(s_cp$excess_1l, s_cp$ndvi_W60d)[1], mm[2, 1]),
                  p_or_se = c(sp(cp$excess_1l, cp$ndvi_W60d)[2], sp(s_cp$excess_1l, s_cp$ndvi_W60d)[2], mm[2, 2]),
                  n = c(nrow(cp), nrow(s_cp), nrow(cp)))
wt(m1b, "m1_root_effect_vs_ndvi.tsv"); print(m1b, digits = 3)

# ---- M2 growth rate -------------------------------------------------------------------------------
gr_file <- file.path(IN, "rep_growth.tsv")
if (!file.exists(gr_file)) { writeLines(log_lines, file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt")); stop("rep_growth.tsv not yet available") }
gr <- rd(gr_file); gr <- gr[is.finite(gr$d_hours) & gr$d_hours > 0, ]
cls <- rd(file.path(IN, "atmo_classification.tsv")); cx <- rd(file.path(IN, "coxL_class.tsv"))
r1l <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1l$", cls$assignment)])
r1h <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1h$", cls$assignment)])
rfI <- unique(sub("\\|.*", "", cx$query[cx$form_I_call == "form_I"]))
tax <- rbind(rd(file.path(IN, "r232_bac120.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2], rd(file.path(IN, "r232_ar53.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2])
rk <- function(cl, p) ifelse(grepl(paste0(p, "__[^;]"), cl), sub(paste0(".*", p, "__([^;]*).*"), "\\1", cl), "unassigned")
gr$phylum <- rk(tax$classification, "p")[match(gr$rep, tax$user_genome)]
gr$order <- paste(gr$phylum, rk(tax$classification, "o")[match(gr$rep, tax$user_genome)], sep = ";")
gr$genus <- paste(gr$order, rk(tax$classification, "g")[match(gr$rep, tax$user_genome)], sep = ";")
gr$c1l <- gr$rep %in% r1l; gr$c1h <- gr$rep %in% r1h; gr$cfI <- gr$rep %in% rfI
gr$logd <- log(gr$d_hours)
say("M2 gRodon estimates for %d representatives (median doubling %.1f h)", nrow(gr), median(gr$d_hours))

# a carriers vs non-carriers, within order
ma <- lmer(logd ~ c1l + c1h + cfI + (1 | order), gr)
fa <- summary(ma)$coefficients
m2a <- data.frame(term = rownames(fa), fa, row.names = NULL,
                  median_d_carriers = c(NA, median(gr$d_hours[gr$c1l]), median(gr$d_hours[gr$c1h]), median(gr$d_hours[gr$cfI])),
                  median_d_noncarriers = c(NA, median(gr$d_hours[!gr$c1l]), median(gr$d_hours[!gr$c1h]), median(gr$d_hours[!gr$cfI])))
wt(m2a, "m2a_doubling_time_by_carrier_status.tsv"); print(m2a, digits = 3)
say("M2a 1l carriers: log doubling time %+.3f within order (SE %.3f); medians %.1f vs %.1f h",
    fa[2, 1], fa[2, 2], m2a$median_d_carriers[2], m2a$median_d_noncarriers[2])

# b community-weighted mean log doubling time per sample (CoverM, reps with an estimate)
ab <- rd(gzfile(file.path(IN, "coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz")), header = FALSE, col.names = c("sample", "rep", "abund"))
ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund) & ab$abund > 0, ]
ab$logd <- gr$logd[match(ab$rep, gr$rep)]
cov_frac <- tapply((!is.na(ab$logd)) * ab$abund, ab$sample, sum) / tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
ab <- ab[!is.na(ab$logd), ]
cwm <- data.frame(sample_id = names(cov_frac), cwm_logd = as.numeric(tapply(ab$logd * ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)[names(cov_frac)] /
                                                     tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)[names(cov_frac)]),
                  covered = as.numeric(cov_frac))
d2 <- merge(d, cwm, by = "sample_id")
say("M2b CWM coverage of mapped abundance: median %.2f", median(d2$covered))
cells <- function(x, a, b, v) { k <- paste(x$site, x$trip); out <- NULL
  for (z in unique(k)) { p <- x[k == z & x$compartment == a, ]; q <- x[k == z & x$compartment == b, ]
    if (nrow(p) && nrow(q)) out <- rbind(out, data.frame(site = p$site[1], trip = p$trip[1], diff = median(p[[v]]) - median(q[[v]]))) }
  out }
db <- cells(d2, "PlantRoot", "Deep", "cwm_logd"); ds <- cells(d2, "Surface", "Deep", "cwm_logd")
sb <- aggregate(diff ~ site, db, median); ss <- aggregate(diff ~ site, ds, median)
m2b <- data.frame(contrast = c("PlantRoot - Deep", "Surface - Deep"), n_pairs = c(nrow(db), nrow(ds)),
                  median_diff_logd = c(median(db$diff), median(ds$diff)),
                  frac_pairs_faster = c(mean(db$diff < 0), mean(ds$diff < 0)),
                  site_wilcox_p = c(wilcox.test(sb$diff)$p.value, wilcox.test(ss$diff)$p.value))
wt(m2b, "m2b_community_doubling_time_shift.tsv"); print(m2b, digits = 3)

# c per-pair 1l excess loss vs the copiotrophy shift
pc <- merge(c1, setNames(db, c("site", "trip", "d_cwm_logd")), by = c("site", "trip"))
mc <- summary(lmer(excess_1l ~ d_cwm_logd + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), pc))$coefficients
mc2 <- summary(lmer(hydB_1l ~ d_cwm_logd + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), pc))$coefficients
m2c <- data.frame(response = c("excess_1l", "excess_1l", "hydB_1l log2FC"),
                  test = c("spearman", "lmer slope per log-unit", "lmer slope per log-unit"),
                  estimate = c(sp(pc$excess_1l, pc$d_cwm_logd)[1], mc[2, 1], mc2[2, 1]),
                  p_or_se = c(sp(pc$excess_1l, pc$d_cwm_logd)[2], mc[2, 2], mc2[2, 2]), n = nrow(pc),
                  intercept_at_no_shift = c(NA, mc[1, 1], mc2[1, 1]))
wt(m2c, "m2c_excess_loss_vs_copiotrophy_shift.tsv"); print(m2c, digits = 3)
wt(merge(pc[, c("site", "trip", "hydB_1l", "excess_1l", "d_cwm_logd")], env[, c("site", "trip", "ndvi_W60d")],
         by = c("site", "trip"), all.x = TRUE), "m_pairs_plantroot_vs_deep.tsv")   # per-pair table for the figure

# d genus level: root depletion ~ growth rate + 1l carriage
mapped <- tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
ab$genus <- gr$genus[match(ab$rep, gr$rep)]
gm <- xtabs(abund ~ sample + genus, ab); gm <- sweep(as.matrix(unclass(gm)), 1, rowSums(gm), "/") * 100
gm <- as.data.frame(gm); gm$sample_id <- rownames(gm); gm <- merge(meta, gm, by = "sample_id")
gen <- setdiff(names(gm), names(meta))
deep_mean <- colMeans(gm[gm$compartment == "Deep", gen]); gen <- gen[deep_mean >= 0.05]
k <- paste(gm$site, gm$trip); keys <- intersect(k[gm$compartment == "PlantRoot"], k[gm$compartment == "Deep"])
lfc <- sapply(gen, function(g) median(sapply(keys, function(z)
  log2((median(gm[[g]][k == z & gm$compartment == "PlantRoot"]) + .01) / (median(gm[[g]][k == z & gm$compartment == "Deep"]) + .01)))))
gt <- data.frame(genus = gen, deep_mean_pct = deep_mean[gen], log2fc_root = lfc,
                 median_logd = tapply(gr$logd, gr$genus, median)[gen],
                 frac_1l = tapply(gr$c1l, gr$genus, mean)[gen], frac_1h = tapply(gr$c1h, gr$genus, mean)[gen],
                 frac_formI = tapply(gr$cfI, gr$genus, mean)[gen], n_reps = as.numeric(table(gr$genus)[gen]))
gt$phylum <- sub(";.*", "", gt$genus)
wt(gt, "m2d_genus_root_depletion.tsv")
fit0 <- lm(log2fc_root ~ median_logd, gt, weights = sqrt(deep_mean_pct))
fit1 <- lm(log2fc_root ~ median_logd + frac_1l + frac_1h + frac_formI, gt, weights = sqrt(deep_mean_pct))
fit2 <- lm(log2fc_root ~ median_logd + frac_1l + frac_1h + frac_formI + phylum, gt, weights = sqrt(deep_mean_pct))
co <- function(f, nm) { s <- summary(f)$coefficients; s <- s[intersect(c("median_logd", "frac_1l", "frac_1h", "frac_formI"), rownames(s)), , drop = FALSE]
  data.frame(model = nm, term = rownames(s), s, r2 = summary(f)$r.squared, n_genera = nrow(gt), row.names = NULL) }
m2d <- rbind(co(fit0, "growth only"), co(fit1, "growth + carriage"), co(fit2, "growth + carriage + phylum"))
wt(m2d, "m2d_genus_models.tsv"); print(m2d, digits = 3)

say("M2b root-adjacent CWM log doubling time shift %+.3f (%.0f%% of %d pairs faster; site p %.2g)",
    m2b$median_diff_logd[1], 100 * m2b$frac_pairs_faster[1], m2b$n_pairs[1], m2b$site_wilcox_p[1])
say("M2c excess_1l vs shift: spearman rho %.2f (p %.2g); lmer slope %.2f (SE %.2f), intercept at no shift %.2f",
    m2c$estimate[1], m2c$p_or_se[1], m2c$estimate[2], m2c$p_or_se[2], m2c$intercept_at_no_shift[2])
f2 <- m2d[m2d$model == "growth + carriage + phylum", ]
say("M2d genus (n=%d): growth slope %.2f (p %.2g); frac_1l %.2f (p %.2g) with phylum",
    nrow(gt), f2$Estimate[f2$term == "median_logd"], f2$Pr...t..[f2$term == "median_logd"],
    f2$Estimate[f2$term == "frac_1l"], f2$Pr...t..[f2$term == "frac_1l"])
writeLines(log_lines, file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt"))

encode complete CBB (RuBisCO + phosphoribulokinase): 0 (0%)
primary fixation pathway encoded: {'rTCA': 525, 'none': 123}
can carry H2-oxidation flux (hydrogenase, biomass>=0): 193 (30%)
can carry CO2-FIXATION flux on trace gases (biomass>=0): 220 (34%)
  fixation reaction carrying the flux (= the mechanism): {'ACL_rTCA': 83, 'OGS_rTCA': 137}
classic biomass-objective autotrophy (grows on air): 0 (0%)

codex
Two checks help prioritise the work. Across 51 bulk-soil sites, group 1l retains its MAP association (Spearman ρ = −0.512), while the individual XRF elements I tested have much weaker associations (|ρ| ≤ 0.203). Also, the existing carbon-fixation and FBA outputs use an older MAG set, so they need reconciliation before supporting a chemosynthesis claim. The dormancy and Kraken2 directories are empty placeholders.

/bin/bash -lc "python3 - <<'PY'
import pandas as pd,numpy as np
from pathlib import Path
from scipy.stats import spearmanr,rankdata
p='lifeonair/results/'

cols=mk+['dna_concentration']+[k+'_scaled' for k in mk];c=z.groupby(['site','trip','compartment'])[cols].median().unstack();c=c.dropna(subset=[('dna_concentration','Deep'),('dna_concentration','PlantRoot')]);print('paired DNA standard median-then-log',len(c),c.index.get_level_values('site').nunique())

# Phase 2 audit

Every number below states the universe it was computed on. Universes are not nested
and are never subtracted from one another.

## Panel

Verified Pfam families: 93 across 8 classes and 57 subclasses.
Rejected accessions: 0 (see `panel/MISSING_ACCESSIONS.tsv`).

## Completeness confounder

Mechanism counts scale with bin completeness. The per-genome CheckM2 completeness is
carried in every matrix so that it can be a covariate in phase 4 rather than a
silent bias. Absence of a class in an incomplete genome is not evidence of absence.

# Robert Hoehndorf scientific writing style: critical adaptation

Source consulted:
`/home/leechuck/Documents/papers/hott/WRITINGSTYLE.md`.

This reference preserves the useful house style while resolving its internal
conflicts and avoiding rules that can reduce accuracy or readability.

## Primary criterion: logical flow of the argument

Robert's explicit priority is the logical flow of the argument; shape comes later
and is secondary. Determine what the passage needs to establish, which premises
and observations support it, and how the conclusion follows. Select and order that
content before deciding on paragraph boundaries or polishing sentences.

For each statement, ask what necessary work it does in this argument. An accurate
fact about the same topic may still be a digression. If removing it leaves the
inference intact and loses no necessary qualification or reproducibility detail,
consider deleting or relocating it. Do not invent a motivation or build an extra
transition simply to retain peripheral material. Preserve necessary intermediate
steps; make missing ones explicit only when the evidence supports them.

Use explanation proportionately to the audience and inference. Defining objects
and operations should make the reasoning follow, without turning every local edit
into a tutorial. A concise deletion can improve logical flow more than several
new explanatory sentences. Evaluate the resulting argument before evaluating its
paragraph shape, rhythm, or resemblance to preferred wording.

## Apply directly

- Prefer active voice when it identifies the responsible actor or makes the
  action clearer. Use passive voice when the actor is irrelevant or the object
  of study should remain the grammatical focus.
- Give readers the context needed to interpret a claim before presenting the
  claim. Do not add a dependent clause mechanically.
- Prefer direct, technically precise sentences. Use a longer multi-clause
  sentence only when the clauses form one inference.
- Calibrate certainty: assert established facts and direct observations;
  use `may`, `can`, or `suggests` for limited or uncertain inferences.
- State what data and results show. Never state what they do not show, mean,
  measure, or imply, and never use "X, not Y" or "X rather than Y" to fence
  off an interpretation the reader has not been given. Write the positive
  statement that carries the information, or delete the sentence.
- Use sentence case for titles and headings unless the venue requires another
  convention.
- Never use em dashes. Replace them with commas, parentheses, colons,
  semicolons, or separate sentences according to the logical relation. In
  LaTeX, do not use `---`.
- Do not use `thus`; use `therefore`.
- Prefix semantic version tags with `v` in prose, for example `PhysioMap
  v1.1.1`.
- Use `record` only for an actual row or serialized data object. For scientific
  content, name the object precisely, for example `axiom`, `assertion`,
  `relation`, `variable`, `value`, or `derivative-sign value`.
- Define every symbol before its first use. When a compact list of formal
  constraints would require readers to decode several new objects at once,
  use a table with one concrete example for each constraint.
- Refer to supplementary material by the shortest specific item, such as a
  subsection, figure, equation, or table. Never use an unqualified `the
  Supplement`. Prefix supplementary item numbers with `S`, and order their
  first citations numerically, for example `Suppl. Fig. S1` before `Suppl.
  Fig. S2`.
- Prefer finite verbs over unnecessary gerunds and nominalizations.
- Preserve domain terminology such as ontology, axiom, embedding, semantic
  similarity, annotation, phenotype, and genotype.
- Use plain verbs where they are equally precise: `use` rather than `utilize`,
  `show` rather than `demonstrate`, `apply` rather than `employ`, `allow` rather
  than `enable`, `reduce` rather than `mitigate`, and `rank` rather than
  `prioritize`.
- Remove editorializers (`fortunately`, `interestingly`, `surprisingly`),
  assertions of obviousness (`clearly`, `obviously`), vague intensifiers
  (`very`, `quite`, `really`), and throat-clearing phrases such as `it is
  important to note`, `in order to`, `a number of`, and `due to the fact that`.
- Use transitions only when they state a real relationship: addition,
  contrast, specification, cause, or consequence.
- Support factual claims with appropriate citations or empirical evidence.
  Report data sources, versions, evaluation splits, sampling, metrics, and
  repository locations when relevant to reproduction.
- Do not print cryptographic digests in a human-facing manuscript or PDF.
  Link to a versioned, machine-readable checksum manifest or metadata file
  that identifies the relevant artifact instead.
- Treat figures as designed scientific arguments rather than collections of
  default plots. In a multi-panel figure, align the panels and use a consistent
  visual grammar for typography, line weights, spacing, panel labels, and
  colour. Give each panel a clear evidential role, remove dead space and
  draft-like annotations, and redesign or separate panels that do not form one
  coherent visual narrative.
- Treat a repository cited by a paper as part of the publication. Its default
  branch and landing page should present the current released resource, its
  semantics, supported operations, limitations, installation, reproducible
  examples, release artifacts, and citation. Keep development history in Git
  history or a changelog rather than making it the repository's public
  narrative. Before claiming availability, verify every link without
  authentication and ensure that the tagged release matches the manuscript.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""PMA per-pair viability QC.

Assesses whether within-ASV PMA signal is consistent enough across the 9 T/UT
pairs to support an ASV-level relic-likelihood indicator. If signal is mostly
noise, Path B is not viable.

Computes:
  - Per-(ASV, pair) T/UT ratios
  - Within-ASV consistency metrics (CV, range, IQR-of-log-ratio)
  - Between- vs within-ASV variance decomposition
  - Pair-pair correlation matrix on log ratios
  - Strata: rhizosphere (6 pairs at C1+C2) vs surface (3 pairs at C2)
  - Stability under thresholding (T/UT<0.1 = relic, >0.5 = alive)
  - Bimodality / mode count of viability distribution

Inputs:
  /home/leechuck/Public/software/empty-quarter/relic-dna/ASV_table_rel_abundance.tsv
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/pma_to_eq_match.tsv

Outputs:
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/qc_per_asv_pair_ratios.parquet
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/qc_per_asv_consistency.tsv
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/qc_pair_pair_corr.tsv
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/qc_summary.txt
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import sys
from pathlib import Path
import re
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import pearsonr, spearmanr

REPO = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
CACHE = REPO / "cache"
RELIC = Path("/home/leechuck/Public/software/empty-quarter/relic-dna")
OUT = CACHE / "test6_disconfirmation"

# Threshold for "detected" in a UT sample -- relative abundance > 1e-5
DETECT_FLOOR = 1e-5
# Min n pairs an ASV must be detected in to be assessed
MIN_PAIRS = 3
# eps for ratio computation
EPS = 1e-7
# ratio clip
RATIO_CLIP = 10.0


def parse_pma_sample(s: str):
    m = re.match(r"^(C[12])([RS])(\d+)(T|UT)$", s)
    if not m:
        return None
    return {"site": m.group(1), "comp_code": m.group(2),
            "rep": int(m.group(3)), "treatment": m.group(4)}


def main():
    print("Loading PMA relative abundance table ...", flush=True)
    rel = pd.read_csv(RELIC / "ASV_table_rel_abundance.tsv", sep="\t",
                      index_col=0)
    print(f"  PMA ASVs x samples: {rel.shape}", flush=True)

    samp = []
    for s in rel.columns:
        m = parse_pma_sample(s)
        if m:
            m["sample_orig"] = s
            samp.append(m)
    sm = pd.DataFrame(samp)
    pairs = (sm.pivot(index=["site", "comp_code", "rep"],
                      columns="treatment", values="sample_orig")
             .dropna(subset=["T", "UT"])
             .reset_index())
    pairs["pair_id"] = pairs.apply(
        lambda r: f"{r['site']}{r['comp_code']}{r['rep']}", axis=1)
    print(f"  T/UT pairs: {len(pairs)}", flush=True)
    print(f"  pair IDs: {list(pairs['pair_id'])}", flush=True)

    # Per (ASV, pair): T, UT, ratio
    print("\nComputing per-(ASV, pair) ratios ...", flush=True)
    rec = []
    for _, p in pairs.iterrows():
        T = rel[p["T"]]; UT = rel[p["UT"]]
        det_mask = UT > DETECT_FLOOR
        for asv_id in rel.index[det_mask]:
            t_val = float(T[asv_id]); ut_val = float(UT[asv_id])
            ratio = (t_val + EPS) / (ut_val + EPS)
            rec.append({
                "asv_id": asv_id,
                "pair_id": p["pair_id"],
                "site": p["site"],
                "comp_code": p["comp_code"],
                "T": t_val,
                "UT": ut_val,
                "ratio": min(ratio, RATIO_CLIP),
                "log_ratio": np.log10(min(ratio, RATIO_CLIP) + EPS),
            })
    pr = pd.DataFrame(rec)
    print(f"  total per-ASV-pair detections: {len(pr)}", flush=True)
    pr.to_parquet(OUT / "qc_per_asv_pair_ratios.parquet")

    # Per-ASV consistency metrics (only ASVs detected in >=MIN_PAIRS)
    print(f"\nComputing per-ASV consistency (min {MIN_PAIRS} pairs) ...",
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""TEST 6C: PMA validation of the ephemeral-OTU finding (revised).

PMA ASVs were derived from a separate DADA2 run with slightly different
primers (RV: GACTACHVGGGTATCTAATCC vs main GGACTACNVGGGTWTCTAAT), so
ASV IDs do not match between datasets. We use vsearch (97% ID) to map
PMA ASVs -> EQ ASVs by sequence similarity.

Then for each EQ ASV with a PMA proxy:
  T:UT relabund ratio per pair (9 pairs, sites C1+C2 trip-5).
  median(T)/median(UT) -> per-EQ-ASV viability score.

Compare viability scores across EQ persistence classes.

Inputs:
  /home/leechuck/Public/software/empty-quarter/relic-dna/ASV_table_rel_abundance.tsv
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/pma_to_eq_match.tsv  (vsearch output)
  cache/test6_persistence/per_OTU_site_persistence.parquet

Output:
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/per_eq_asv_viability.tsv
  cache/test6_disconfirmation/test6c_summary.txt
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import sys
from pathlib import Path
import re
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import mannwhitneyu, kruskal

REPO = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
sys.path.insert(0, str(REPO / "scripts"))

CACHE = REPO / "cache"
RELIC = Path("/home/leechuck/Public/software/empty-quarter/relic-dna")
OUT = CACHE / "test6_disconfirmation"


def parse_relic_sample(name: str):
    m = re.match(r"^(C[12])([RS])(\d+)(T|UT)$", name)
    if not m: return None
    site, comp, rep, treat = m.groups()
    return {"site": site, "comp_code": comp, "rep": int(rep),
            "treatment": treat}


def main():
    rel = pd.read_csv(RELIC / "ASV_table_rel_abundance.tsv", sep="\t",
                      index_col=0)
    samp = []
    for s in rel.columns:
        m = parse_relic_sample(s)
        if m: m["sample_orig"] = s; samp.append(m)
    sm = pd.DataFrame(samp)
    pairs = sm.pivot(index=["site", "comp_code", "rep"],
                      columns="treatment", values="sample_orig").dropna(
                          subset=["T", "UT"])
    print(f"PMA T/UT pairs: {len(pairs)}", flush=True)

    # PMA ASV -> EQ ASV mapping from vsearch
    map_df = pd.read_csv(OUT / "pma_to_eq_match.tsv", sep="\t", header=None,
                          names=["pma_asv", "eq_asv", "pid", "alen", "mismatches",
                                 "gaps", "qstart", "qend", "tstart", "tend",
                                 "evalue", "bitscore"])
    print(f"PMA->EQ map: {len(map_df)} rows ({map_df['pma_asv'].nunique()} unique PMA, "
          f"{map_df['eq_asv'].nunique()} unique EQ)", flush=True)

    # Per pair, compute T/UT log-ratio per PMA ASV

#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
# Life on air (P3): compact environmental inputs for H1-H3, frozen as TSV with checksums.
# Sources (amplicon repository cache; sha256 recorded in lifeonair/results/inputs/env_sources.sha256):
#   climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet  daily ERA5 T and P per site (Open-Meteo, 0.25 deg)
#   metadata_with_rainfall.parquet        per amplicon sample: trip_date (sampling date)
#   ndvi_by_sample.parquet, smap_by_sample.parquet   MODIS NDVI (60 d) and SMAP soil moisture
#   srtm_topo.parquet                     site lat, lon, elevation
# Metagenome sample ids equal the amplicon ids without the "eNNNN_" prefix.
# Outputs: inputs/env_site.tsv (site climate normals), inputs/env_site_trip.tsv (sampling date,
# antecedent precipitation, NDVI/SMAP per site x trip).
#
#   Rscript lifeonair/code/prep_environment.R

suppressMessages(library(arrow))
C   <- path.expand("~/Documents/papers/empty-quarter-amplicon/repository/cache")
IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
rd  <- function(f) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", stringsAsFactors = FALSE, check.names = FALSE)

clim <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet")))
clim$date <- as.Date(clim$date)
clim$year <- as.integer(format(clim$date, "%Y"))
ann <- aggregate(P ~ site + year, data = clim, FUN = sum)
site <- data.frame(site = sort(unique(clim$site)))
site$map_era5_mm  <- tapply(ann$P, ann$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
site$mat_era5_c   <- tapply(clim$T, clim$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
site$rainy_days_per_yr <- tapply(clim$P >= 1, clim$site, sum)[as.character(site$site)] / length(unique(clim$year))
topo <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "srtm_topo.parquet")))
site <- merge(site, topo, by = "site", all.x = TRUE)

md <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet")))
md$sample_id <- sub("^e[0-9]+_", "", md$sample)
md$trip_date <- as.Date(md$trip_date)
nd <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "ndvi_by_sample.parquet")))
sm <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "smap_by_sample.parquet")))
md <- merge(md, nd, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE); md <- merge(md, sm, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE)

# antecedent ERA5 precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end)
ante <- function(s, d, days) {
  x <- clim[clim$site == s & clim$date < d & clim$date >= d - days, ]
  if (d - 1 > max(clim$date)) return(NA_real_)
  sum(x$P)
}
# per site x trip: sampling date (earliest amplicon date in that cell) and site-level remote sensing
st <- aggregate(trip_date ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = min)
st$trip_date <- as.Date(st$trip_date, origin = "1970-01-01")
rs <- aggregate(cbind(ndvi_W60d, evi_W60d, smap_sm_W30d) ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = median, na.action = na.pass)
st <- merge(st, rs, by = c("site", "trip"), all.x = TRUE)
# antecedent ERA5 precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end)
st$P90_era5  <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 90)
st$P365_era5 <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 365)
st$trip <- paste0("trip", st$trip)

write.table(site, file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
write.table(st, file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
src <- file.path(C, c("climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet", "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet",
                      "ndvi_by_sample.parquet", "smap_by_sample.parquet", "srtm_topo.parquet"))
writeLines(vapply(src, function(f) system2("sha256sum", f, stdout = TRUE), ""), file.path(IN, "env_sources.sha256"))
mg <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
cov <- sum(paste(mg$site, mg$trip) %in% paste(st$site, st$trip))
cat(sprintf("sites %d; site x trip cells %d (P365 available %d); metagenome samples covered %d of %d\n",
            nrow(site), nrow(st), sum(!is.na(st$P365_era5)), cov, nrow(mg)))

 firrincieli_2026_genomic-evidence-aerotrophy-defining.fulltext.xml
AbstractMembers of the class Ktedonobacteria (phylum Chloroflexota) are widespread across various terrestrial environments, including oligotrophic caves, although the genomic basis underlying this distribution remains unclear. Here, we present a systematic genomic analysis of Ktedonobacteria across ecosystems, with a focus on oligotrophic caves. Cave Ktedonobacteria belong to novel genera within the Ktedonobacteraceae and harbour genes associated with a mixotrophic metabolism combining the use of organic and inorganic substrates as energy and carbon sources. Comparative analyses of all available Ktedonobacteria genomes from diverse environments showed that most metabolic traits, including those associated with atmospheric gas oxidation, are primarily conserved among members of the same family. In contrast, genes involved in CO₂ fixation are enriched in Ktedonobacteria inhabiting caves. Phylogenetic analysis indicated that the RuBisCO of Ktedonobacteria likely represents a novel Form I subtype (named group IG) encompassing thermophilic and acidophilic bacteria from six different phyla that often inhabit similar extreme environments. The presence of this subtype across distinct lineages in comparable habitats suggests that it may confer a selective advantage in nutrient-poor settings (like caves) and that its distribution may be influenced by horizontal gene transfer. This inferred autotrophic capacity is associated with a transaldolase variant of the Calvin–Benson–Bassham cycle that was previously described only in a single Firmicutes species. Overall, this study provides genetic evidence for the potential coupling of atmospheric gas oxidation with dark CO₂ fixation in Ktedonobacteria, highlighting their possible role in sustaining primary production in oligotrophic ecosystems, including caves.

 medinacaro_2026_metabolic-transitions-along-moisture.fulltext.xml
BackgroundThe Barrancas Blancas (BB) plain, situated in the high-altitude Atacama region, is a cryogenic, hyper-arid, poly-extreme environment. The seasonal formation of a temporary freshwater lake under these harsh conditions provides a unique opportunity to examine how liquid water influences the metabolic responses of desert microbial communities. Using a metatranscriptomic approach, we characterised microbial community responses along a 70-meter natural moisture gradient.ResultsAlong the gradient, the most active sites (8–23 m from the lake) exhibited high RNA recovery and diverse metabolic functions. Bacillariophyta (diatoms) drove oxygenic phototrophy, while Pseudomonadota contributed to anoxygenic phototrophy and nitrogen fixation. Additionally, Pseudomonadota, Actinomycetota, and Bacteroidota expressed genes for the oxidation of nitrate, sulfide, thiosulfate, and trace gases (H₂, CO). The energy derived from these processes was reflected in the high capacity for carbon fixation by these taxa. Moreover, network analysis revealed that these primary producers co-occurred with a diverse range of heterotrophic prokaryotic and eukaryotic groups. In contrast, at the driest site, hydrogen oxidation was the primary energy-conserving process, predominantly associated with Actinomycetota, which also contributed to hydrogenotrophic carbon fixation. Notably, even at this site, heterotrophic eukaryotes co-occurred with these chemolithotrophic primary producers.ConclusionsThis study presents the first transcriptomic analysis from the high-altitude Atacama Desert, facilitated by the favourable moisture conditions. Furthermore, these findings highlight a moisture-driven transition in microbial energy acquisition strategies and emphasise the ecological significance of both photoautotrophy and chemolithotrophy, which likely vary depending on the dynamics of temporary lakes. The BB plain and its lake thus offer a robust model for understanding microbial resilience, functional plasticity, community assembly, and trophic interactions in extreme environments, providing novel insights into life at the edge of habitability.Graphical abstract Supplementary InformationThe online v

 southern-africa_2024_dryland-functional-redundancy.fulltext.xml
AbstractDrylands’ poly-extreme conditions limit edaphic microbial diversity and functionality. Furthermore, climate change exacerbates soil desiccation and salinity in most drylands. To better understand the potential effects of these changes on dryland microbial communities, we evaluated their taxonomic and functional diversities in two Southern African dryland soils with contrasting aridity and salinity. Fungal community structure was significantly influenced by aridity and salinity, while Bacteria and Archaea only by salinity. Deterministic homogeneous selection was significantly more important for bacterial and archaeal communities’ assembly in hyperarid and saline soils when compared to those from arid soils. This suggests that niche partitioning drives bacterial and archaeal communities' assembly under the most extreme conditions. Conversely, stochastic dispersal limitations drove the assembly of fungal communities. Hyperarid and saline soil communities exhibited similar potential functional capacities, demonstrating a disconnect between microbial structure and function. Structure variations could be functionally compensated by different taxa with similar functions, as implied by the high levels of functional redundancy. Consequently, while environmental selective pressures shape the dryland microbial community assembly and structures, they do not influence their potential functionality. This suggests that they are functionally stable and that they could be functional even under harsher conditions, such as those expected with climate change.
